1	# Caw
2	
3	Caw pushes PR feedback to the agent that raised the PR, so the agent fixes its own PR without being prodded. GitHub webhooks (failing checks, review comments, mergeability) are compiled into one summary and delivered over a held-open SSE connection to the listening agent — or held as pending for the next agent to pick up. Harness-agnostic (any MCP client) and reviewer-agnostic (any commenting bot or human).
4	
5	## Language
6	
7	**Session**:
8	The agent that raised the PR — Claude, Gemini, OpenAI, or any MCP-capable harness. The SSE client.
9	_Avoid_: agent (overloaded), cloud agent, client.
10	
11	**Hub**:
12	The backend that receives GitHub webhooks, compiles them, persists state, serves SSE, and runs the one poll. A portable Go + SQLite service — the same artifact whether self-hosted or run as the SaaS.
13	_Avoid_: server, backend, API.
14	
15	**Watcher**:
16	The local MCP server running inside the harness. Holds the SSE connection to the Hub and renders summaries (label + symbol + colour) into the Session.
17	_Avoid_: plugin, client, agent.
18	
19	**Subscription**:
20	The link between a Session and a PR, keyed `owner/repo#number` and bound to that Session's live SSE connection. Present = originator is listening; absent = orphaned.
21	_Avoid_: connection, channel.
22	
23	**Round**:
24	One settle-cycle for a single PR head SHA (`owner/repo#number @ sha`). A new push opens a new Round and supersedes the prior Round's unacked items.
25	_Avoid_: run, cycle, batch.
26	
27	**Signal-type**:
28	The three kinds of feedback Caw surfaces — **Checks**, **Comments**, **Mergeability**. Sources within each are attributed dynamically, never hardcoded.
29	_Avoid_: category, event type.
30	
31	**Pending item**:
32	A compiled summary for a PR with no live Subscription, stored by the Hub until a Session retrieves it via the one-shot startup check.
33	_Avoid_: queued event, backlog.
34	
35	## Relationships
36	
37	- A **Session** opens one **Subscription** per PR it raises; the **Subscription** owns one held-open SSE connection.
38	- The **Hub** ingests GitHub webhooks for a **Round** and emits exactly one compiled summary per Round per PR.
39	- A summary carries one or more **Signal-types**; **Comments** and **Checks** attribute their **source** dynamically.
40	- **Mergeability** is the only signal produced by a poll (after a Round settles), never by a webhook.
41	- No live **Subscription** → the summary becomes a **Pending item**; the next **Session** drains it via a single startup request.
42	
43	## Example dialogue
44	
45	> **Dev:** "When CI fails on a PR the **Session** raised, does Caw poll GitHub?"
46	> **Domain expert:** "No. The **Watcher** is holding an SSE to the **Hub**; the **Hub** got the failure as a webhook and pushes the compiled summary down that connection. The *only* poll in the whole system is the **Mergeability** re-verify, once a **Round** settles."
47	
48	> **Dev:** "What if the **Session** already closed?"
49	> **Domain expert:** "Then there's no live **Subscription**, so the summary is stored as a **Pending item**. The next agent that starts makes one request — 'anything pending?' — sees it, and asks the human before working on it. That single request isn't polling; it happens once at startup."
50	
51	> **Dev:** "A PR is behind base but has no conflicts — who rebases?"
52	> **Domain expert:** "If a **Session** is listening, the **Session** rebases in its own worktree. If it's orphaned, the **Hub** rebases as a fallback. Either way auto-merge is set after."
53	
54	## Flagged ambiguities
55	
56	- "agent" was used to mean the Session, the Watcher, and the Hub all at once — resolved into three distinct terms.
57	- "cloud" / "cloud agent" meant the Anthropic Claude Agent session — resolved to **Session**.
58	- "subscribe to the agents" implied the Hub subscribes to Sessions — resolved: **Sessions subscribe to the Hub**; direction is always GitHub → Hub → SSE → Watcher → Session.
59	- "polling" was used for three different things — resolved: the *only* poll is the Mergeability re-verify; live delivery is SSE push; the startup pending-check is a single one-shot request, not polling.
60	- Channels (Claude Code research-preview push) — deferred; not part of v1. Will return as an optional per-harness push lane once it reaches general release. The portable contract is MCP either way.
61	
```

1	# Caw
2	
3	> Push PR feedback to the agent that raised the PR — so it fixes its own PR without being prodded.
4	
5	When an AI agent raises a pull request, getting it green (CI, review comments, mergeability) usually means a human babysitting the PR and re-prompting the agent every time something fails. Caw inverts that: GitHub webhooks are compiled into a single summary and **pushed** to the agent over a held-open SSE connection, so the agent that raised the PR keeps working it on its own. If that agent is gone, the summary waits as a *pending item* for the next agent to pick up.
6	
7	Caw is **harness-agnostic** (any MCP client — Claude, Gemini, OpenAI, Cursor) and **reviewer-agnostic** (any commenting bot or human — CodeRabbit, Sonar, Snyk, …).
8	
9	See [`CONTEXT.md`](./CONTEXT.md) for the canonical glossary and [`docs/adr/`](./docs/adr/) for the load-bearing decisions.
10	
11	## How it works
12	
13	```
14	GitHub ──webhooks──▶ Hub ──compile──▶ SSE push ──▶ Watcher ──▶ Session (listening agent)
15	                      │                                              fixes in worktree, pushes
16	                      └─ no listener? store as Pending item ──▶ next Session's startup check ──▶ prompt human
17	```
18	
19	**Live path.** A **Session** raises a PR; its **Watcher** (a local MCP server) opens and *holds* an SSE connection to the **Hub** keyed `owner/repo#number`. The Hub ingests that **Round**'s GitHub webhooks; once checks settle (`check_suite completed` + ~30s grace) it runs the one poll (mergeability), compiles **one** summary, and pushes it down the held connection. The Session acts in the PR's worktree, pushes, and the next Round begins. A new push supersedes the prior Round's unacked items (state is keyed by head SHA).
20	
21	**Catch-up path.** No live Subscription (Session closed) → the summary is stored as a **Pending item**. A brand-new Session makes **one** request at startup ("anything pending?"), surfaced by a SessionStart hook, and prompts the human before acting on work it didn't start.
22	
23	**The only poll in the whole system** is the mergeability/conflict re-verify after a Round settles. Everything else is webhook push (GitHub → Hub) and SSE push (Hub → Watcher). The startup pending-check is a single one-shot request, not polling.
24	
25	## Signal-types
26	
27	Feedback is surfaced as three dynamic signal-types; sources within each are attributed at runtime, never hardcoded.
28	
29	| Signal-type | GitHub source | Notes |
30	|---|---|---|
31	| **Checks** | `check_suite` failures | CI, Squawk-as-check, Sonar-as-check… |
32	| **Comments** | `pull_request_review`, `pull_request_review_comment`, `issue_comment` | Any bot or human; severity normalised for first-class sources (CodeRabbit first) |
33	| **Mergeability** | poll of `GET /pulls/{n}` after settle | Behind-base & clean → Session rebases; orphan → Hub rebases; then auto-merge |
34	
35	Severity renders as **label + symbol + colour** (e.g. `■ CRITICAL` / `▲ MAJOR` / `· minor`), never colour alone — readable on colourblind-accessible terminal themes and on no-colour terminals.
36	
37	## Components
38	
39	### Hub (Go + SQLite)
40	One portable service; identical artifact self-hosted or run as SaaS ([ADR-0001](./docs/adr/0001-portable-go-sqlite-hub-over-cloudflare.md)).
41	
42	- **Webhook ingress** — `POST /webhooks/github`, verifies `X-Hub-Signature-256`, dedupes, buckets by `owner/repo#number @ sha` (Round).
43	- **Round settle** — on `check_suite completed` + grace window, run the mergeability poll, compile the summary.
44	- **SSE** — `GET /sse/{owner}/{repo}/{number}` (authenticated); pushes compiled summaries to a held connection.
45	- **Pending store** — SQLite; summaries with no live subscriber persisted until acked. `GET /pending` (one-shot startup check), `POST /ack`.
46	- **Rebase fallback** — for orphaned PRs only: "Update branch" + enable auto-merge ([ADR-0002](./docs/adr/0002-agent-owned-rebase.md)).
47	
48	### Watcher (MCP server)
49	Local, runs in the harness; the universal contract across harnesses.
50	
51	- `subscribe_pr(owner, repo, number)` — opens & holds the SSE for the Round; returns compiled summaries as they arrive.
52	- `get_pending()` — one-shot; returns unacked Pending items across the user's PRs.
53	- `ack(key)` — marks a summary handled.
54	- Renders summaries (label + symbol + colour). SessionStart hook reminds the agent to call `get_pending()`.
55	
56	### GitHub App
57	Auth for both modes via the App Manifest flow ([ADR rationale in CONTEXT](./CONTEXT.md)).
58	
59	- **SaaS:** hosted App; user clicks *Install*, picks repos. No stored PATs.
60	- **Self-host:** Manifest flow provisions the user's own App pointing at their Hub.
61	- Events: `check_suite`, `pull_request`, `pull_request_review`, `pull_request_review_comment`, `issue_comment`.
62	- Permissions (pin exact scopes at build): `pull_requests:read/write`, `contents:write`, `checks:read`.
63	- The **local agent keeps its own `gh`/git creds** — the App is the Hub's identity only.
64	
65	## Deploy modes
66	
67	- **Self-host:** `docker run` the Hub, run the Manifest flow, point the Watcher at your Hub URL.
68	- **SaaS:** install the hosted App, install the Watcher, subscribe. (Pricing TBD.)
69	
70	## Build slices (suggested order)
71	
72	1. **Hub core** — webhook ingest + signature verify + Round bucketing + SQLite pending store.
73	2. **Compile + SSE** — `check_suite`+grace settle, summary compiler, SSE push, `get_pending`/`ack`.
74	3. **Mergeability poll** — settle-time poll; emit the Mergeability signal.
75	4. **Watcher MCP** — `subscribe_pr` / `get_pending` / `ack`, summary rendering, SessionStart hook.
76	5. **GitHub App + Manifest** — App, install/manifest flows, installation tokens.
77	6. **Rebase** — Session-side rebase guidance + Hub orphan fallback + auto-merge.
78	7. **Severity** — CodeRabbit parser → normalised severity; label/symbol/colour rendering.
79	
80	## Open questions (parked for build time)
81	
82	- Exact GitHub App permission scopes.
83	- CodeRabbit comment format → severity parsing (and the next first-class source).
84	- SSE auth: how the Watcher proves it owns `owner/repo#number` (tie to App installation / a per-Session token).
85	- Grace-window duration (start ~30s; tune).
86	- Channels: re-add as an optional Claude push lane once it reaches general release.
87	
88	## Status
89	
90	Pre-implementation. Design converged; see `CONTEXT.md` + ADRs. No code yet.
91	
```

1	# 0001 — Portable Go + SQLite Hub over Cloudflare-native
2	
3	**Status:** Accepted
4	**Date:** 2026-06-07
5	
6	## Decision
7	
8	The Hub is a single portable service — Go + embedded SQLite (optional Postgres at SaaS scale), plain SSE — packaged as one artifact that runs identically whether a user self-hosts it or we operate it as the SaaS. We are **not** building the Hub on Cloudflare Workers + Durable Objects + D1.
9	
10	## Why
11	
12	Self-hosting is a first-class product option: a user must be able to `docker run` their own Hub and point their Watcher at it. Durable Objects and D1 are Cloudflare-proprietary and cannot be self-hosted, so a CF-native Hub would force a second, separate backend for self-hosters — two codebases, drifting features, double the bugs. One portable artifact keeps "self-host or subscribe" a deploy-time flag rather than two products, and matches existing Go expertise.
13	
14	## Trade-offs accepted
15	
16	- We give up Durable Objects' built-in per-entity connection-holding and durable state. Holding SSE connections with SQLite-backed state is a well-understood Go problem, so this is a modest cost.
17	- The SaaS runs the same image at scale (behind Cloudflare as CDN/tunnel, not as the app runtime), so we operate a stateful service rather than leaning on serverless autoscaling.
18	
19	## Considered alternatives
20	
21	- **Cloudflare Worker + DO + D1 (SaaS-only):** rejected — breaks first-class self-host.
22	- **Two backends (CF for SaaS, Go for self-host):** rejected — two codebases to maintain in lockstep.
23	
```

1	# 0002 — Agent-owned rebase, Hub rebases only orphans
2	
3	**Status:** Accepted
4	**Date:** 2026-06-07
5	
6	## Decision
7	
8	When a PR is mergeable-but-behind-base with no conflicts, the **listening Session** performs the rebase in its own worktree and pushes. The **Hub** performs a server-side rebase only as a fallback for **orphaned** PRs (no live Subscription). Auto-merge (`--auto --squash`) is set by whichever actor acted.
9	
10	## Why
11	
12	A Hub force-pushing a rebased branch can race with work the local agent or human is doing on that same branch head — a real, previously-experienced failure mode (commits lost to branch trampling when the working directory is switched out from under an actor). The listening Session is the only actor that knows whether it is mid-edit and owns its worktree, so it is the safe place to rebase. Orphaned PRs have no local actor to trample, so the Hub can rebase them without risk.
13	
14	## Trade-offs accepted
15	
16	- The Hub needs `contents:write` on the GitHub App for the orphan fallback path even though it rarely uses it.
17	- Rebase behaviour differs by whether a Session is listening — slightly more logic than "Hub always rebases," accepted to eliminate the trampling race.
18	
19	## Considered alternatives
20	
21	- **Hub always rebases server-side:** rejected — force-push races with live local work; reintroduces the trampling failure.
22	- **Never auto-rebase, always report only:** rejected — loses the hands-off value for orphaned PRs that just need a clean rebase.
23	
```

> AGENT

I've read all four artifacts. The design is genuinely converged — the glossary is sharp and the ADRs capture real trade-offs. So I won't re-litigate settled ground. Instead I'll walk the decision tree starting at its **root**, where I think there's an unresolved tension that everything else (SSE auth, the product pitch, the pending model) hangs off.

Let me start.

---

## Q1 — Is the Session the *originator*, or just *a listener*?

Your `CONTEXT.md` defines **Subscription** as "the link between a Session and a PR... **Present = originator is listening**; absent = orphaned." And the whole pitch (README line 3) is "push PR feedback to the agent that **raised** the PR."

But the *mechanism* is `subscribe_pr(owner, repo, number)` — any Session can call that with any PR number. Nothing in the design proves the subscribing Session is the one that authored the PR. And your **Pending item** path already concedes this: a Pending item is drained by "the **next** Session," explicitly *not* the originator — and that next Session "asks the human before working on it."

So there are really two different identity models fighting:

- **(A) "Originator" model** — the binding is *provable authorship*. The Hub somehow knows Session X raised PR #42, and only X gets the live push. This justifies "fixes its *own* PR without a prompt" — but it's hard: an MCP server in a harness has no durable identity across the GitHub App, and you'd need to correlate "who pushed the branch" with "who opened the SSE."

- **(B) "Listener" model** — the binding is just *whoever currently holds the SSE for that key*. Simpler, matches the code surface exactly, and the pending/ask-the-human path already assumes it. But then "the agent that *raised* the PR" is marketing, not a guarantee — and your autonomy rule ("a listening originator continues its already-approved task **without a prompt**") leaks: if the listener isn't provably the originator, why does it get to skip the human prompt that orphan-drainers must show?

**My recommendation: (B), the listener model — but tighten the language to make it honest.** In practice the originator *is* the listener almost always (same harness session that just ran `gh pr create` calls `subscribe_pr` moments later), so you get the product promise without needing to *prove* authorship. The safety distinction that actually matters isn't "originator vs. stranger" — it's **"is this work already inside an approved task context?"** A live subscription opened in the same session that's mid-task → continue without prompt. A pending item picked up cold by a fresh session → prompt the human. That's a property of *session continuity*, not *authorship*.

This means: drop "originator" from the Subscription definition, redefine it as "the Session currently holding the SSE for this key," and re-found the autonomy rule on *live-subscription-in-session* rather than *raised-it*.

**Where do you land — (A) provable originator, (B) listener-with-honest-language, or do you see a third framing?** And if (B): does the autonomy distinction hold up as "session continuity" rather than "authorship"?

> DEVELOPER

A, why is it risky? i'm sure every cluade agent session has a unique id, use that for originator is easily identifier, also if sse connection drops or closed then add it to pending for that repo, next time any agent in that repo location opens a new agent, it will check for all the pending unresolved PR

> AGENT

Good — you're committing to (A). Let me stress-test it, because I think the session ID buys you less than it looks like, and it collides with one of your own documented constraints. One probe at a time.

## Q2 — Walk me through how the **Hub** learns that "session `abc-123` raised PR #42."

Trace the actual wire path against your own design:

1. The Session runs `gh pr create` using **its own git/gh creds** (HANDOFF line 36: "local agent keeps its own `gh`"). GitHub records the PR author as the **GitHub user login** — not the Claude session ID.
2. The Hub finds out a PR exists via **webhook**. That webhook payload contains `owner/repo#number` and the GitHub author login. It contains **nothing** about `abc-123`. The Hub has no channel through which a session ID could ever reach it from GitHub.
3. So the only way the Hub ever associates `abc-123` with #42 is if **the Session itself tells it** — presumably on the `subscribe_pr` call ("I'm `abc-123`, I raised #42").

That last step is the problem: **a self-asserted session ID is a *name*, not a *credential*.** Any client can call `subscribe_pr(owner, repo, 42, claimed_session="abc-123")`. The session ID doesn't *prove* origination — it just *asserts* it. So you still need a real SSE-auth mechanism underneath (the open question #1 you flagged), and once you have that, the session ID isn't doing the security work — the auth token is.

Two more things break, both against your own docs:

- **Harness-agnostic.** CONTEXT/README promise "any MCP client — Claude, Gemini, OpenAI, Cursor." "Every Claude session has a unique ID" is Claude-specific. Gemini/OpenAI sessions don't expose the same thing. If *originator identity* is founded on a Claude session ID, the core identity model is no longer portable — it's a Claude feature wearing a harness-agnostic label.

- **Session IDs don't survive a restart — so "originator" evaporates.** When the human closes the terminal and starts a fresh Claude session tomorrow, it's a *new* session ID. The session that "raised #42" is gone forever. So originator-identity is only meaningful *within a single live session's lifetime* — and **within that window, the originator is by definition the one still alive holding the SSE.** Across sessions, you're always on the pending path anyway (which you described correctly — "next agent in that repo checks pending").

Here's the punchline I want you to push back on: **the PR key `owner/repo#number` is already a unique routing key.** At most one live SSE per key. So "route to the originator session" and "route to whoever holds this PR's stream" are *operationally identical* — the session ID changes nothing about *who gets the push*. It only changes *who is allowed to open the stream*, and for that a session ID is a weak credential.

**My recommendation stands, refined:** keep the *concept* of originator for the product story, but found the *mechanism* on **(1) a per-PR/per-installation auth token for SSE ownership** (the real security) and **(2) `owner/repo#number` as the routing key** (already unique). Use the session ID, if a harness exposes one, only as an *opportunistic continuity hint* for the autonomy decision (continue-without-prompt vs. ask-human) — never as the security boundary or the routing key.

**So:** what does the session ID give you that `owner/repo#number` + an auth token doesn't? If the answer is "nothing for routing, and it's not a credential for auth" — do you still want it as the *primary* originator key, or demote it to a continuity hint?

> DEVELOPER

offcourse when the session is raising the PR, it will itself start the SSE which can inform our hub that a session with id ex 123 wants to associate with a PR it raised ex #453, now when hub receives events for #453 it reverse checks with session id 123 which sse associated

> AGENT

Notice what just happened: the flow you described **is the listener model wearing the originator's name** — and it quietly proves my point rather than refuting it.

Trace your own steps:
- Session opens SSE → "I'm `123`, associate me with #453"
- Hub stores: `#453 → (the held connection)`
- Event for #453 arrives → Hub pushes down that connection

The Hub routes by **`#453`**, because that's the key the *event* carries. It never needs to "reverse-check session 123" — it looks up `#453 → connection`. Strip the string `"123"` out entirely and replace it with the raw connection handle, and **nothing about routing changes.** So in your own mechanism the session ID is a *label on the socket*, not a routing key and not a credential. That's the demotion I proposed — you've arrived at it.

But your mechanism has a hole it doesn't close, and it's the exact open question your HANDOFF says to "nail before build slice 2":

## Q3 — What stops a *different* client from opening the SSE for #453 and claiming it?

Your association is **self-asserted, first-come**: whoever opens a socket and says "associate me with #453" gets the stream. So:

- A buggy second session, a retry, or a malicious client calls `subscribe_pr(owner, repo, 453, "I'm session 999")`. On what basis does the Hub accept or reject it? Session `123` was never a secret — it can't be the gate.
- **Collision:** two of *your own* sessions both claim #453 (a retry after a crash; a concurrent session). First-writer-wins locks out the legitimate reconnect. Last-writer-wins lets the second steal the stream. Which rule, and why?
- **Transient drop vs. orphan:** you said "if SSE drops → pending." But a flaky network where *the same session* reconnects 2 seconds later should *not* become a Pending item with a human prompt — that's a spurious interruption. The Hub can only tell "same session reconnecting" from "new session" if the reconnecter **re-presents something stable.** *This* is the one job the session ID actually earns: a continuity token for reconnect, not a security boundary.

**My recommendation:**

1. **Authorize stream-open by GitHub App installation, not by session ID.** The Hub *already* receives `owner/repo`'s webhooks because the App is installed there — so it already trusts "this installation owns this repo." Make the Watcher present a token derived from that same installation (or a short-lived per-PR token the Hub mints when it first sees the PR). "Can you open the stream for `owner/repo#453`?" becomes "do you hold a token proving you're the installation that owns `owner/repo`?" — **GitHub-verifiable**, which a session ID never is. This also stays harness-agnostic: it's GitHub identity, not Claude identity.

2. **Session ID = continuity hint only.** Present it on reconnect so a transient drop reclaims the live stream instead of falling to pending; use it for the continue-without-prompt vs. ask-human autonomy call. If a harness doesn't expose one, the system still works — it just always treats a reconnect as a fresh subscribe.

3. **Collision rule:** within one authorized installation, **last-writer-wins for the live socket, prior socket gets a "superseded" close**; the prior session's unacked work survives as a Round/pending item. (Same-user sessions are mutually trusted, so theft isn't the threat there — staleness is.)

**So, the two things I need you to actually decide here:** (a) Is SSE-open authorization tied to the **GitHub App installation / a Hub-minted per-PR token** — yes or do you have another credential in mind? (b) On a same-PR collision between two authorized sessions: **last-writer-wins**, or first-writer-holds?

> DEVELOPER

well in the sql row, we can have a column like auto gen uuid v7, session id, pr number, repo name, org id, github username etc. and use session-id and pr number as a combined unique key, so that if anyone else tries to claim it, it will fail via constraint

> AGENT

That schema instinct is right (uuid v7 PK is a fine surrogate, time-ordered — keep it). But the **composite unique key you picked does the exact opposite of what you want.** This is the sharpest thing in the whole thread, so let me slow down on it.

## Q4 — `UNIQUE(session_id, pr_number)` *permits* the theft you're trying to block.

Think about what a unique constraint actually enforces: it rejects a second row with **the same tuple**. Now run your attacker through it:

- Legit row exists: `(session=123, pr=453)`.
- Attacker calls subscribe claiming `session=999`. They insert `(session=999, pr=453)`.
- `(999, 453)` ≠ `(123, 453)` → **different tuple → constraint is satisfied → insert succeeds.**

You now have **two rows owning PR #453**, and the constraint did nothing. Including `session_id` in the key is precisely what *allows* many sessions per PR. The constraint only fires if the *same* session re-claims the *same* PR — which is the one case (reconnect) you actually want to *allow*, not block.

To enforce "one owner per PR," the unique key must be the **PR identity alone**:

```
UNIQUE(org_id, repo_name, pr_number)
```

No `session_id` in it. But now notice the second trap: with that key, a **legitimate reconnect or takeover also hits the constraint and fails** — same problem from the other side. So a raw constraint can't tell "steal" from "reconnect." That's because:

**A unique constraint guards *integrity* (no duplicate rows). It is not *authorization* (proving you're allowed).** Two different jobs. Even with `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)`, the *first* writer wins — and if a stranger races you and wins, the constraint now **locks you out of your own PR**. You've turned the constraint into a DoS weapon. The constraint can't distinguish friend from foe because nothing in the INSERT is *authenticated*.

And `org_id` / `github_username` in the row — if those are **self-asserted on the subscribe call**, a stranger just lies about them. They're only trustworthy if **derived from a verified GitHub App installation token**, which drags us right back to the auth question from Q3 that's still open.

**My recommendation — three moves:**

1. **Unique key = `UNIQUE(org_id, repo_name, pr_number)`.** The Subscription is one-owner-per-PR. `session_id` becomes a *non-key column* on that row.
2. **(Re)subscribe is an authenticated UPSERT**, not a blind insert. Authorization comes from a **GitHub App installation token** (the Hub already trusts that the installation owns the repo — it's receiving its webhooks). On a valid call, you *overwrite* `session_id` + the connection handle. Reconnect and last-writer-wins both become "update the row," which the constraint happily allows. A stranger with no valid installation token never gets to the UPSERT at all.
3. **`org_id` / `github_username` are populated from the verified token, never from the request body.**

So the constraint enforces "one owner," and **auth — not the constraint — enforces *who* that owner may be.**

**Decision I need from you:** do you accept `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)` + auth-gated UPSERT (session_id demoted to a plain column / continuity hint)? Or do you still want `session_id` in the uniqueness key — and if so, how do you stop the `(999, 453)` insert that slips right past it?

> DEVELOPER

even though it's unlikely but if multiple session wants to listen, let them, how does it matter?

> AGENT

It matters because of one thing your own ADR-0002 already settled: **in Caw, a Subscription isn't passive listening — it's a claim to *act*.** The listening Session doesn't just read the summary; it edits the PR, rebases in its worktree, and pushes. So "let multiple sessions listen" means "let multiple sessions *act on the same branch*" — which is the precise failure ADR-0002 was written to kill.

## Q5 — Concrete scenario: PR #453 fails CI, sessions A and B are both subscribed.

The Hub compiles one summary ("Checks failed: 3 lint errors") and pushes it down **both** held connections. Now:

- A reads it, edits `foo.go`, fixes the lints its way, pushes → SHA-x.
- B reads the *same* summary, edits `foo.go` differently, rebases, pushes → SHA-y.
- One of those pushes loses the other's commits.

That is verbatim ADR-0002: *"commits lost to branch trampling when the working directory is switched out from under an actor."* You eliminated this race between the Hub and a Session — and "let them all listen" **reintroduces it between two Sessions**, self-inflicted.

It also dissolves three invariants you've already written down:

1. **Orphan detection.** CONTEXT: "Present = listening; absent = orphaned." With N listeners, "orphaned?" stops being a boolean on the PR — it's a *count*. The pending → next-session path loses its pivot.
2. **Ack.** If A acks "handled" but B is mid-fix on the old Round, is it handled or not? Double-fix, or dropped work.
3. **Round supersede.** A's push opens a new Round and supersedes the old one — while B is still working the *superseded* Round. B's effort is now wasted and its push conflicts.

So "how does it matter" = it matters precisely where it costs the most: concurrent writers on one branch.

**But there's a legitimate version of your instinct** — separate the *roles*:

- **One acting owner per PR** (the `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)` lease from Q4). Exactly one actor. This preserves ADR-0002 and the orphan/ack/Round invariants.
- **Any number of read-only observers**, if you ever want them (a human watching a dashboard while the agent fixes). Observers receive the summary but **never act, never rebase, never ack-to-resolve.**

**My recommendation:** keep the **single-actor lease** as a hard invariant, and **don't build observer fan-out in v1** (YAGNI — no concrete consumer yet; the open questions list is already long). If a dashboard need appears later, add it as an explicitly read-only lane that can't violate the single-actor rule. And sharpen the glossary: **Subscription = the *acting* lease for a PR, not mere listening** — that one word ("acting") is what makes ADR-0002 hold.

**Decision:** single-acting-owner (observers deferred) — or do you genuinely want multiple *actors*, and if so, how do you reconcile that with ADR-0002's trampling race on the shared branch?

> DEVELOPER

let it fanout

> AGENT

Fine — fan-out it is. But "let it fanout" hides a fork, and exactly one branch of it corrupts data. Let me pin which fan-out you mean, because git itself already settles most of this for you.

## Q6 — Fan-out the *notification* is free. Fan-out the *rebase* is the one that loses commits.

Two different things get "fanned out," and they have opposite safety profiles:

- **Normal commits (the common fix path).** If A and B both fix #453 and push, **git protects you for free**: A's push lands, B's push is a **non-fast-forward → rejected**. B doesn't silently lose A's work — B gets an error and must re-pull. So fan-out here costs *wasted work* (N agents fix, N−1 get bounced) but **never corrupts**. Acceptable.

- **Rebase (force-push).** This is the *only* path with no git-level guard. A rebase is `push --force-with-lease`/`--force`, which **overwrites** history. Two actors rebasing the same branch = one set of commits gone. This is verbatim ADR-0002 — and it's the one case where git won't save you, because force-push is *defined* as "overwrite the other guy."

So fan-out is safe everywhere **except rebase**. That's the line.

**My recommendation — fan-out, with one carve-out:**

1. **Fan-out the compiled summary** to every live listener. Cheap, harmless, matches your instinct.
2. **Fan-out normal fix pushes** — let git's non-fast-forward rejection be the arbiter. Document that a rejected push means "someone beat you; re-pull and re-evaluate," so agents handle the bounce gracefully instead of force-pushing over it.
3. **Keep rebase single-owner** — a per-PR *rebase lease* (the `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)` row, now scoped to *"who may force-push"* rather than *"who may listen"*). Exactly one actor rebases; this preserves ADR-0002 intact.
4. **Redefine the bookkeeping for N listeners:** orphan = *listener-count == 0* (not absent-boolean); ack = *first-ack-clears-pending* (any listener acking marks the summary handled).

This gives you the fan-out you asked for *and* keeps ADR-0002's guarantee, by narrowing the single-owner constraint from "listening" down to just "force-pushing."

This reverses the scope of two of your written artifacts, so once you confirm I'll update them inline: **CONTEXT.md** (Subscription becomes "any number of listeners; one rebase lease" and orphan becomes count-based) and **ADR-0002** (amend: single-owner now applies to *rebase*, not to subscription).

**Decision:** accept fan-out-everything-**except**-rebase-stays-single-owner? Or do you want **rebase to fan out too** — in which case ADR-0002 is explicitly overturned and you're accepting the force-push trampling race as a known cost?

> AGENT

That's a clean decision: **fan-out the summary to every listener; git's non-fast-forward rejection arbitrates ordinary fixes; the rebase (force-push) stays single-owner.** I'll take your "that's fine" as accepting that rebase carve-out — say so if you meant rebase should fan out too.

Docs updated (CONTEXT.md glossary + relationships + ambiguities; ADR-0002 amended). Note: README still describes the Subscription as singular ("opens & holds *the* SSE") in a few spots — I'll fix those once we've finished grilling, so they don't drift.

Now the open question your own HANDOFF marks "**nail before build slice 2**" — and fan-out actually *simplifies* it.

## Q7 — SSE auth: what authorizes opening a stream for `owner/repo#N`?

Here's why fan-out makes this easier: you no longer need to enforce *exclusivity* on the stream (you're allowing many listeners). So SSE auth collapses from "prove you're *the* owner of this PR" down to a pure **repo-scoped visibility check**: *"are you allowed to see this repo's PR feedback at all?"*

The clean answer is the credential the Hub **already trusts**: the **GitHub App installation**. The Hub receives `owner/repo`'s webhooks *because* the App is installed there — so the Hub already holds the mapping `installation → repos`. Make the Watcher present a token proving it acts for that installation; the Hub checks the requested `owner/repo` is in that installation's repo set. No PATs (matches HANDOFF line 36), harness-agnostic (GitHub identity, not Claude identity), and self-host/SaaS symmetric.

The wrinkle to decide: **how does the Watcher get that token?** Two realistic shapes —

- **(i) Hub-minted per-subscriber token.** During install/Manifest the Hub hands the user a Hub credential (e.g. an API key tied to their installation). The Watcher sends it on `subscribe_pr`/`get_pending`/`ack`. Simple, works for any harness, and the Hub fully controls revocation. Downside: one more secret the user stores in their MCP config.
- **(ii) GitHub user OAuth on the Watcher.** The Watcher does a GitHub OAuth device flow; the Hub verifies the user has access to `owner/repo` via the API. No Hub-specific secret, but adds an OAuth dance to every Watcher and an API round-trip per subscribe.

**My recommendation: (i) — a Hub-minted per-installation API token**, issued at install time (SaaS: when they click *Install*; self-host: when the Manifest flow provisions their App). It's the least moving parts, gives you clean server-side revocation, and the user already does a one-time install step where handing over a token costs nothing extra. Reserve (ii) for later if someone needs per-user (not per-installation) granularity inside a shared org.

One consequence to accept: a per-installation token means **anyone in that installation can subscribe to any of its PRs' streams.** With fan-out that's already your posture (you're not protecting PRs from same-org siblings — only from strangers), so it's consistent. Cross-user isolation *within* an org would need (ii); I don't think v1 needs it.

**Decision:** (i) Hub-minted per-installation token, (ii) GitHub OAuth on the Watcher, or a third option? And do you accept "any member of the installation can listen to any of its PRs" as the v1 isolation boundary?

> DEVELOPER

recommendation

> AGENT

Going with (i). This one clears the ADR bar — hard to reverse (it shapes the install flow, the Watcher config, and the Hub's auth surface), surprising without context (a reader will ask "why not GitHub OAuth?"), and a real trade-off. Recording it:

> AGENT

ADR-0003 created — that closes your "nail before slice 2" open question. Now to the correctness-critical core your HANDOFF flags as the test-friendly heart of the system: **Round settle**.

## Q8 — A CodeRabbit comment arrives 90 seconds *after* the Round already settled and the summary was pushed. What happens to it?

Your settle rule is "`check_suite completed` + ~30s grace → poll mergeability → compile **one** summary per Round." The design is explicit that you deliberately *don't* wait for all three signal-types, because "Squawk only fires on migration PRs; CodeRabbit is async — a barrier would hang" (HANDOFF line 33). Correct call. But it creates the inverse problem: the async signal you refused to wait for **arrives late, after you've already emitted the Round's one summary.**

Walk the cases:

- CI completes at T+0, grace ends T+30, summary pushed. CodeRabbit posts its review at T+90. The head SHA hasn't changed — so by your key (`owner/repo#number @ sha`) this is **still the same Round**, which has already emitted its "exactly one" summary. So is the comment:
  - **(a) dropped** — violates "surface all feedback";
  - **(b) appended to the same Round as a second push** — violates "exactly one summary per Round";
  - **(c) opens a follow-up emission within the same Round** — needs the "one summary per Round" invariant relaxed to "one *per settle-cycle*, Rounds can re-settle"?

This is the real tension: **"one summary per Round" assumes all inputs arrive before settle, but you intentionally settle before the async ones arrive.** Those two can't both hold.

**My recommendation: (c), and relax the invariant to "one summary per *settle event*, and a Round can settle more than once."** Concretely:

- A Round settles and emits when its **grace timer fires after the last-seen check_suite completion**. That's settle #1.
- A *late signal on the same SHA* (comment, or a check that flipped) **re-arms the grace timer** and produces settle #2 — a fresh compiled summary for the same Round, marked as a follow-up (e.g. a monotonically increasing `seq` within the Round). Each push down the SSE is self-contained.
- A **new head SHA** still supersedes the whole Round as designed (the agent pushed a fix; old unacked items for the prior SHA are moot).

So the invariant becomes: *exactly one summary per settle; SHA change supersedes; same-SHA late input re-settles.* This keeps your "don't barrier on async reviewers" decision **and** never drops a late comment.

Trade-off you accept: an agent can receive **two summaries for the same SHA** (the CI one, then the CodeRabbit one ~minute later). With fan-out + git arbitration already in place that's fine — the agent handles the second the same way it handles any Round.

The thing to tune is the **re-arm window**: how long after settle #1 will you still accept a late same-SHA signal as a re-settle vs. ignore it? I'd cap it (e.g. accept late signals for N minutes after the head SHA was first seen; after that, only a new SHA reopens work) so a comment posted hours later on an abandoned PR doesn't wake an agent.

**Decision:** accept (c) — "one summary per *settle*, Rounds can re-settle on same-SHA late input, SHA change supersedes"? And what's your re-arm cap — 5 min? 15? Tied to PR still being open?

A late signal whose SHA is no longer the head → superseded. PR closed/merged → stop. And a late comment with *no live listener* simply becomes a **Pending item** (drained at next startup with a human prompt) — so the "comment posted hours later" case can't spuriously wake a fresh agent anyway. The only one it reaches is a still-live long-running session, where the ping is legitimately useful. So SHA + open-state are the bounds; no arbitrary minutes to tune.

A late signal arriving on the **same head SHA** (an async review comment, a check that flipped state) **re-arms the grace timer and re-settles** the Round, emitting a fresh summary tagged with a monotonically increasing `seq`. A **new head SHA** opens a new Round and supersedes the prior Round's unacked items.\n\nRe-settle is bounded by **SHA-currency and PR-open state, not a timer**. Acceptable: with fan-out and git's non-fast-forward arbitration (ADR-0002), an agent already handles each summary independently.\n- Slightly more state per Round (last-settle `seq`, re-arm tracking) than a one-shot settle.\n\n## Considered alternatives\n\n- **Barrier on all signal-types before settling:** rejected — async/absent reviewers hang the Round.\n- **One-shot settle, drop late same-SHA signals:** rejected — silently loses real review feedback.\n- **Hard re-arm time cap (N minutes):** rejected as unnecessary — SHA-currency + PR-open + the Pending path already bound it without a tunable magic number.\n"
}

> AGENT

Two ADRs and the glossary now reflect the settle model. Next branch — and this one is a gap our **own fan-out decision just opened**, so it's a direct dependency to resolve:

## Q9 — With fan-out, *N* listeners all receive "Mergeability: behind base, clean → rebase needed." Who actually rebases?

Before fan-out this was trivial: one listener, it rebases. Now the rebase lease (ADR-0002 amended) says *at most one* actor force-pushes — but you've got N listeners who all just got the same "please rebase" signal. So the lease needs an **acquisition protocol**, and there's a handoff race with the Hub's orphan fallback:

1. **Claiming among listeners.** Three listeners see "rebase needed." If there's no coordination, all three rebase and force-push → the exact trampling ADR-0002 forbids, now back via the lease being unenforced. So the lease must be *granted by the Hub*, not assumed by the listener. Something like: a listener that wants to rebase calls `acquire_rebase_lease(key)`; the Hub grants it to exactly one (DB row, `UNIQUE(org,repo,pr)` — the one place that constraint genuinely belongs); the others get "denied, someone's rebasing" and stand down.

2. **The orphan handoff race.** The Hub rebases *only* when there are zero listeners. But "zero listeners" and "a listener exists" flip in real time. Sequence: Hub sees listener-count drop to 0 → decides to rebase as orphan fallback → meanwhile a fresh Session subscribes and *also* decides to rebase. Now Hub (force-push) and Session (force-push) race — ADR-0002's original Hub-vs-Session trampling, resurrected. So the **Hub must acquire the same lease** before its orphan rebase, through the same single-grant path, and a late-arriving listener must check the lease before rebasing rather than assuming it's free.

3. **Lease release / staleness.** The lease-holder crashes mid-rebase. The lease is now held by a dead actor; the PR is stuck behind base forever. So the lease needs a **TTL / heartbeat** and a reclaim path.

**My recommendation:**

- **Hub-granted, single-holder lease.** The actor that intends to force-push calls `acquire_rebase_lease(key)`; the Hub grants to exactly one via the `UNIQUE(org,repo,pr)` row (this is what that constraint is *for* — not subscription). Losers stand down and just wait for the next Round (the rebaser's push opens a new SHA → new Round → everyone re-syncs). This holds whether the contenders are N listeners or the Hub itself.
- **One unified path for listener *and* Hub.** The Hub's orphan rebase isn't special-cased — it acquires the same lease. So the "zero listeners" decision and a late subscriber both funnel through one grant point; no Hub-vs-Session race possible.
- **TTL + heartbeat on the lease** (say 60–120s, renewed while rebasing); on expiry the Hub reclaims and either re-grants or does the orphan rebase itself. This is the *only* place I'd keep an actual timer (a crashed force-push genuinely needs a deadline — SHA-currency can't detect a hung actor).

This keeps ADR-0002's guarantee intact under fan-out by making the lease **explicitly granted**, never assumed.

**Decision:** Hub-granted single-holder lease (same path for listeners and the orphan Hub fallback), losers stand down, TTL+heartbeat for crash recovery? Or do you want a different arbiter — e.g. let everyone try and lean on `--force-with-lease` to fail all-but-one at the git layer?

> DEVELOPER

recommendation

{
  "file_path": "/Users/jobinlawrance/Project/caw/docs/adr/0005-hub-granted-rebase-lease.md",
  "content": "# 0005 — Hub-granted rebase lease (single grant point for listeners and the orphan fallback)\n\n**Status:** Accepted\n**Date:** 2026-06-07\n\n## Decision\n\nThe right to force-push a rebased branch is an explicit **lease granted by the Hub**, never assumed by an actor. Any actor intending to rebase — a listening Session, or the Hub itself for an orphan — calls one grant point that hands the lease to **exactly one** holder via the `UNIQUE(org_id, repo_name, pr_number)` row. Losers stand down and wait for the next Round (the winner's force-push opens a new head SHA → new Round → everyone re-syncs). The lease carries a **TTL with heartbeat** (~60–120s, renewed while rebasing); on expiry the Hub reclaims it and re-grants or performs the orphan rebase itself.\n\n## Why\n\nFan-out (ADR-0002 amendment) means *N* listeners can receive the same \"behind base → rebase\" mergeability signal simultaneously. Without an explicit grant they would all force-push and trample each other — the exact failure ADR-0002 exists to prevent, re-introduced *between listeners*. Making the lease Hub-granted reduces \"who rebases?\" to a single atomic decision.\n\nRouting the Hub's orphan fallback through the **same** grant point closes the original Hub-vs-Session race too: the \"zero listeners → Hub rebases\" decision and a late-arriving subscriber that also wants to rebase both contend for one lease, so they can never both force-push.\n\nThe `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)` constraint — explicitly *not* the right tool for subscription exclusivity (subscriptions fan out) — is exactly right here: the rebase lease is the one genuinely single-owner resource in the system.\n\nA TTL+heartbeat is the **only** timer we keep, because a crashed lease-holder mid-rebase cannot be detected by SHA-currency or PR-open state — a hung actor needs a deadline.\n\n## Trade-offs accepted\n\n- An extra round-trip (`acquire_rebase_lease`) before a rebase. Negligible: rebase is already a heavyweight, infrequent operation.\n- Lease bookkeeping (holder, TTL, heartbeat, reclaim) is real state the Hub must manage.\n\n## Considered alternatives\n\n- **Let everyone rebase, rely on `git push --force-with-lease`:** rejected. It prevents *silent* overwrite (the second push fails if the ref moved), but it does not prevent wasteful concurrent rebases, leaves messy per-actor failure handling, and — critically — cannot coordinate the **Hub-vs-Session** orphan race, since they are different actors with independent local state. Hub-granted leasing solves all three.\n- **Listener self-elects (e.g. lowest session ID rebases):** rejected — requires every listener to know the full listener set and agree, which the fan-out model does not give them; the Hub already knows.\n"
}

> AGENT

Lease recorded (ADR-0005) and the glossary points to it. Next — another interaction our own decisions created, between ADR-0004 (re-settle) and the Pending store:

## Q10 — When no one's listening, does a PR pile up *multiple* Pending items, and what clears stale ones?

Re-settle (ADR-0004) means one SHA can emit summary `seq=1` (CI), then `seq=2` (CodeRabbit). Now drop all listeners. Each settle with zero listeners becomes a Pending item. So:

1. **Accumulation.** Does PR #453 now hold *two* Pending items (`seq=1`, `seq=2`) for the same SHA? The next Session's `get_pending()` would surface both — but `seq=2` is a *superset re-compile* of the same Round; showing both is redundant and confusing ("two failures?" when it's one evolving Round). I think a later same-SHA settle should **supersede** the earlier Pending item, not stack on it — Pending holds **at most one item per `owner/repo#number`, always the latest seq.**

2. **Stale-SHA pending.** A Pending item exists for SHA-x. The human manually pushes SHA-y (fixes it outside Caw, or another tool). The SHA-x Pending item is now moot but still sitting there — the next Session gets prompted about feedback for a SHA that no longer exists. So Pending items must be **invalidated when the head SHA moves** (same supersede rule as Rounds) — or at minimum the drain path must check "is this still the head SHA?" before prompting.

3. **Closed/merged PR.** Feedback was pending; the PR gets merged or closed. The Pending item is now noise. Should be **dropped on `pull_request closed/merged`**.

4. **Unbounded growth / abandonment.** A PR is opened, gets feedback, abandoned, never acked, never closed. Its Pending item lives forever. Across an active org that's slow unbounded growth in the store. Needs a **TTL or a cap** — though I'd lean on event-driven invalidation (close/merge/new-SHA) first and treat a TTL as a backstop only.

**My recommendation:**

- **One Pending item per PR, latest `seq` wins** — a re-settle *replaces* the prior pending row (upsert keyed `owner/repo#number`), never stacks.
- **Event-driven invalidation:** new head SHA supersedes; `pull_request closed/merged` deletes. These reuse webhooks you already ingest — no new polling.
- **TTL as backstop only** (e.g. drop Pending items untouched for N days) to bound abandoned-PR growth, since not every abandoned PR emits a close event.
- **Drain re-validates:** `get_pending()` (or the Hub before returning it) confirms the SHA is still the head and the PR still open; anything stale is filtered, not shown.

This keeps the "prompt the human about real, current work" promise and stops the store growing without bound.

**Decision:** one-pending-per-PR-latest-seq + event-driven invalidation (SHA-move, close/merge) + TTL backstop + drain-time re-validation? Or do you want every settle preserved as a separate Pending item (full history) instead of collapse-to-latest?

> AGENT

Clear call: the Hub is a **latest-state store**, not a relevance engine — that's the consumer's job. Let me record it, but I'm going to flag one consequence you're implicitly accepting and add one small thing so it doesn't bite.

**The consequence of "nothing clears":** a PR merged three weeks ago whose Checks/Comments items were never replaced will be returned by *every* future `get_pending()` forever. Over months, a fresh session's startup check returns a growing pile of dead-PR items it must re-evaluate each time. You said relevance is the consumer's concern — fair — but the consumer can only judge "is this PR still open / is this still the head SHA?" if the Hub *hands it that data*. So the one addition I'm baking in: **each Pending item carries its `timestamp`, head `SHA`, and PR state (open/closed/merged)** — the Hub still clears nothing, but the agent can cheaply skip the dead ones. That keeps your stance intact and removes the noise objection.

This also makes **`ack` redundant**: with "latest same-type event replaces," acting on a PR generates new GitHub events that overwrite the old pending naturally — there's nothing for an explicit ack to clear. I'll drop `ack` from the model (flagging README for the same fix).

Recording:

  "content": "# 0006 — Pending is a latest-state store per signal-type; the consumer owns relevance\n\n**Status:** Accepted\n**Date:** 2026-06-07\n\n## Decision\n\nThe Pending store holds, per `owner/repo#number` **per signal-type**, the **latest** compiled summary for that type. A newer same-type event **replaces** the stored one (keyed by timestamp); the store is latest-state, not an append log and not a history. The Hub does **not** garbage-collect Pending items — nothing expires them by age, by `pull_request closed/merged`, or by head-SHA move. They persist until overwritten by a newer same-type event.\n\nA starting Session's one-shot `get_pending()` returns **all** current items for its installation. Choosing which to act on, and how (serially, in parallel, or skip), is the **consumer's** responsibility, not the Hub's. To make that judgment cheap, every Pending item carries its `timestamp`, head `SHA`, and PR state (open/closed/merged).\n\nThere is no `ack`: because acting on a PR produces new GitHub events that overwrite the relevant same-type Pending item, an explicit acknowledgement has nothing to clear.\n\n## Why\n\nThe Hub should not encode policy about what work matters — different harnesses and humans will triage differently. Keeping the Hub a dumb latest-state store keeps it simple and harness-agnostic, and pushes triage to the agent/human who actually has context. \"Latest per type\" gives a clean, bounded-per-PR snapshot (not a confusing stack of partial re-settles from ADR-0004) while still never dropping a distinct signal-type.\n\nReturning the SHA and PR state with each item lets the consumer filter dead items (merged PR, stale SHA) without the Hub making that call — so \"nothing clears\" does not translate into startup noise.\n\n## Trade-offs accepted\n\n- **Unbounded store growth in principle:** abandoned PRs that never close and never get a newer event keep their items forever. Accepted deliberately; if it becomes a real operational problem, a TTL backstop can be added later without changing the contract (it would only drop items the consumer would already filter as stale).\n- The consumer must do its own relevance filtering on every startup. Accepted — it is the only actor with the context to do it well, and the per-item metadata makes it cheap.\n\n## Considered alternatives\n\n- **Event-driven invalidation (delete on close/merge/new-SHA) + TTL:** rejected as the default — it bakes relevance policy into the Hub. Kept in reserve as a pure backstop if growth bites.\n- **Append-log of every settle (full history):** rejected — surfaces redundant partial re-settles (ADR-0004) and grows far faster, for history no consumer asked for.\n- **Explicit `ack` to clear items:** rejected as redundant — new GitHub events from acting already overwrite the item.\n"
}

Docs are now internally consistent. Here's where the decision tree stands:

**Resolved & recorded (6 ADRs + sharpened glossary):**
- **Identity/fan-out** — listeners fan out; routing by PR key; session-ID rejected as ownership key (CONTEXT *Flagged ambiguities*).
- **SSE auth** — Hub-minted per-installation token (ADR-0003) → closes your "nail before slice 2" blocker.
- **Settle** — one summary *per settle*; same-SHA late signals re-settle (ADR-0004).
- **Rebase** — single-owner *lease*, Hub-granted, TTL+heartbeat (ADR-0002 amend, ADR-0005).
- **Pending** — latest-state per PR per type, nothing clears, consumer owns relevance, no `ack` (ADR-0006).

**Remaining open questions are all genuinely build-time/tunable** (the docs now park them honestly): exact App scopes, grace-window & lease-TTL values, Channels re-add. None warrant an ADR yet.

**The one remaining branch with real design shape** is severity:

## Q11 — How do arbitrary sources map onto your fixed severity scale?

You've decided the *rendering* (`■ CRITICAL` / `▲ MAJOR` / `· minor` — label+symbol+colour, never colour alone). But the *mapping* is undecided: CodeRabbit emits its own severity vocabulary, Sonar another, a raw failing check has none, a human comment has none. Something must normalise N source vocabularies → your fixed ladder.

**My recommendation:** a small **per-source adapter** model — each first-class source (CodeRabbit first) has a parser that maps its vocabulary to your ladder; everything else falls through to a default (e.g. checks → `MAJOR`, bare human/bot comments → `minor`, unparseable → `minor`). Keep the ladder closed (3–4 levels) and the adapters open/pluggable — which matches your "sources attributed dynamically, never hardcoded" principle: the *ladder* is fixed, the *adapters* are the dynamic part. This is reversible (add adapters later), so it's README-parked, not an ADR.

**Do you want to grill the severity mapping now, or are we at shared understanding?** If the latter, your next actions are unchanged from HANDOFF — commit the scaffold, create the GitHub repo, `/to-issues` the build slices — and I'd suggest committing the doc updates from this session first.

## What changed this session

Six design decisions were stress-tested and either reversed or hardened, then captured *inline*:

| Branch | Resolution | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Originator identity | Rejected session-ID-as-key; **listeners fan out**, route by PR key | CONTEXT *Flagged ambiguities* |
| SSE auth | **Hub-minted per-installation token** (was your slice-2 blocker) | ADR-0003 |
| Concurrent actors | Fan-out summaries; git arbitrates pushes; **rebase = single-owner lease** | ADR-0002 (amended) |
| Late async signals | **One summary per *settle*; same-SHA re-settles** | ADR-0004 |
| Who rebases under fan-out | **Hub-granted lease**, one grant path, TTL+heartbeat | ADR-0005 |
| Pending semantics | **Latest-state per type, nothing clears, consumer owns relevance, no `ack`** | ADR-0006 |
| Severity | Closed ladder, open adapters | README |

The biggest structural shift: **fan-out** rippled through four other decisions (auth simplified to repo-scoped, the `UNIQUE(org,repo,pr)` constraint moved from subscription to the rebase lease, `ack` became redundant, orphan became count-based). All consistent now across CONTEXT.md, README.md, and ADRs 0002–0006.

**Files touched:** `CONTEXT.md`, `README.md`, `docs/adr/0002` (amended), and new `0003`–`0006`.

## Genuinely still open (build-time, no ADR needed)
- Exact GitHub App permission scopes
- Grace-window (~30s) & rebase-lease TTL (~60–120s) — tune empirically
- CodeRabbit parser specifics + next first-class source
- Channels re-add once it GAs

{
  "file_path": "/Users/jobinlawrance/Project/caw/docs/adr/0007-subscriptions-fan-out.md",
  "content": "# 0007 — Subscriptions fan out; the PR key is the only router\n\n**Status:** Accepted\n**Date:** 2026-06-07\n\n## Decision\n\nAny number of Sessions may hold a Subscription to the same PR. The Hub pushes each compiled summary to **all** live connections for `owner/repo#number` (fan-out). Routing is by the **PR key alone** — at most one logical stream per key, delivered to every listener on it. There is no \"originator\" identity enforced at the Hub: typically the Session that raised the PR is among the listeners, but that is neither proven nor required.\n\nThe only single-owner resource in the system is the **rebase lease** (ADR-0005). Ordinary concurrent pushes need no coordination — git's non-fast-forward rejection arbitrates them.\n\n## Why\n\nAn MCP Watcher has no durable, harness-portable identity the Hub can verify, and the Hub only ever learns of a PR through GitHub webhooks (which carry the GitHub author, never a harness session id). So \"deliver only to the agent that raised the PR\" cannot be *enforced* without inventing a credential. Rather than build that machinery for a guarantee that buys nothing operationally, we fan out: the PR key is already a unique router, and in practice the raiser is the listener.\n\nFan-out also collapses three other problems:\n- **SSE auth** drops from \"prove exclusive ownership of this PR\" to a repo-scoped visibility check (ADR-0003).\n- The `UNIQUE(org, repo, pr)` constraint is freed from subscriptions and put where it belongs — the rebase lease.\n- **Concurrency** is safe by default: git rejects non-fast-forward pushes, so a losing concurrent fixer simply re-pulls; only force-push (rebase) needs the lease.\n\n## Trade-offs accepted\n\n- Two Sessions deliberately pointed at one PR will both receive every summary and may both attempt fixes — wasted effort, but no corruption (git arbitrates; rebase is leased). This is a user choice, not a failure mode the Hub prevents.\n- \"The agent that raised the PR\" becomes the *typical* case, not a *guaranteed* one — the product story is honest about this (CONTEXT.md → Flagged ambiguities).\n\n## Considered alternatives\n\n- **Single-owner subscription keyed by a session id:** rejected — a self-asserted session id is a name, not a credential, and is harness-specific (breaks harness-agnostic); it adds nothing to routing the PR key doesn't already give.\n- **Single-owner subscription with a real per-PR token:** rejected — exclusivity solves a problem fan-out doesn't have, at the cost of reconnect/handoff complexity.\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Confirmed from its docs: **OpenTUI is a TypeScript library for building *terminal UIs*** — components, layout, React/Solid bindings (the TUI core is even written in Zig). It *draws interfaces*. That matters because it surfaces a category mismatch I want to resolve before re-opening ADR-0001:

**The Hub has no UI.** It's a headless service: receives GitHub webhooks over HTTP, holds open SSE connections, persists state in SQLite, ships as one `docker run`-able artifact for self-hosters. OpenTUI can't *be* the Hub — a TUI renderer and an HTTP/SSE/SQLite backend aren't substitutes. So "Go vs OpenTUI" isn't actually a choice on the same axis.

So I think one of three things is really going on, and they lead to very different places:

- **(A) You'd rather write the *backend* in TypeScript than Go.** Legitimate — and it *does* re-open ADR-0001. But the real alternative there is **Bun**, not opentui: `bun:sqlite` (embedded), native `Response` SSE streaming, and `bun build --compile` → a single self-hostable binary. That satisfies ADR-0001's *actual* load-bearing requirement (one portable artifact, self-host or SaaS). The only thing ADR-0001 cited *for* Go that Bun doesn't answer is "existing Go expertise" — so this comes down to: which language do *you* want to live in, and is a young runtime acceptable for a long-lived connection-holding server?

- **(B) You want a human-facing TUI dashboard** to *watch* PRs/Rounds from the terminal. That's a genuinely useful but **net-new component** not in the current design (which is agent-facing via MCP). OpenTUI is a fine pick *for that* — and it changes nothing about the Hub or ADR-0001.

- **(C) You want the *Watcher* in TS with opentui rendering.** But the Watcher is an MCP server — it returns summaries as **text into the agent's own terminal**; it doesn't draw its own UI. So opentui would have no surface to render on here unless (B) is also true.

My recommendation: opentui doesn't displace the Hub regardless. If the driver is "TS over Go," reframe it as **Go-vs-Bun for the backend** and I'll grill that on ADR-0001's terms. If the driver is "I want a TUI to watch this," that's **(B)** — worth adding, orthogonal to the Hub.

> AGENT

Good — opentui's off the table for the backend; the real question is **Go vs Kotlin + Spring Boot for the Hub.** Let me judge it strictly against what *this* service is, because the workload is unusually opinionated.

The Hub is a **lean, stateful daemon**: hold thousands of long-lived SSE connections, ingest webhooks, embedded SQLite, and — per ADR-0001's *core* driver — ship as **one artifact a user can `docker run` to self-host.** That last constraint does most of the deciding.

## Scored against ADR-0001's requirements

| Requirement | Go | Kotlin + Spring Boot |
|---|---|---|
| **Self-host artifact** | Single static binary (~15 MB, `modernc.org/sqlite` = no cgo), distroless image ~20 MB, sub-second start, ~tens of MB RAM | Fat JAR needs a JRE (image ~250–400 MB, Spring context ~200 MB+, multi-second start). GraalVM native fixes size/speed but adds AOT/reflection-config build pain |
| **Hold many SSE connections** | Goroutine-per-connection (~few KB each) — the canonical Go strength; `net/http` SSE is trivial | Servlet = thread-per-connection (~1 MB, pool-bound) → you *must* go **WebFlux/reactive (Netty)** to hold them cheaply |
| **Embedded SQLite** | First-class, single file, pure-Go driver | Works via `sqlite-jdbc`, but it's **blocking JDBC** — which fights WebFlux's reactive stack (you'd want R2DBC or careful offloading), and SQLite is second-class in Spring land |
| **One codebase, self-host = SaaS** | Same tiny binary everywhere | Same JAR, but heavy for a self-hoster on a small VM |

The trap is the interaction: to hold SSE cheaply you pick **WebFlux**, but your data layer (embedded SQLite/JDBC) is **blocking** — so you inherit reactive complexity *and* an impedance mismatch, to land where Go is by default.

## Recommendation: **stay with Go.**

This service is exactly Go's sweet spot — a small, connection-holding network daemon you want to hand someone as a single binary. Spring Boot's real strengths (huge ecosystem, DI, JPA, enterprise patterns, big-team scaling, complex domain logic) are things **this Hub doesn't need**, and its costs (JVM footprint / GraalVM build friction, reactive-or-bust SSE, SQLite-as-afterthought) all push *against* first-class self-host.

**Kotlin/Spring would be the right call if** any of these flip: the Hub grows into a business-logic-heavy app; you're a Kotlin-first team that won't maintain Go; you want the JVM/GitHub-library ecosystem; or self-host quietly stops being first-class and you standardize on Postgres + a heavier deploy. None of those are true today.

**One honest caveat:** if *you personally* are far more fluent in Kotlin/Spring than Go, "best language for the workload" and "best language for *you* to ship and maintain this" can diverge — and for a solo/small project, shipping velocity often wins. ADR-0001 cited "existing Go expertise," so I've assumed Go fluency holds.

**So:** lock Go and I'll amend ADR-0001 to record this Go-vs-Kotlin/Spring comparison explicitly (right now it only argues Go-vs-Cloudflare — a future reader will ask "why not Spring?"). Or — is your Kotlin fluency strong enough that maintainability should override the workload fit?

> DEVELOPER

lets go with go but also use gin framework

> AGENT

Go + Gin — good, and Gin is a sound pick: you get router + middleware (signature-verify, auth, logging, recovery) for the webhook/REST surface (`POST /webhooks/github`, `GET /sse/...`, `GET /pending`, the lease endpoints) without much ceremony. Two things to keep in mind, both about the Hub's defining workload — **held-open SSE**:

1. **SSE in Gin is a manual streaming handler**, not a route convenience. The pattern is `c.Stream(func(w io.Writer) bool { ... })` driven by your per-subscription channel, with `c.Writer.Flush()` after each event and disconnect detection via `c.Request.Context().Done()`. Gin's value is the *middleware stack around* the SSE route, not the streaming itself.
2. **Don't let middleware buffer the stream.** Any response-buffering/gzip middleware on the SSE route will swallow the held connection — exempt `/sse/...` from those, and set `X-Accel-Buffering: no` so a fronting proxy (your Cloudflare-as-CDN per ADR-0001) doesn't buffer it either.

Gin is reversible (it's an internal HTTP layer behind `http.Handler`), so it doesn't need its own ADR. I'll fold "Go, Gin as the HTTP layer" into ADR-0001 and record the **Kotlin/Spring + Bun comparison** there too, since that's the part a future reader will question.

## Resolved: backend stack
- **Language: Go** — best fit for this workload (lean, connection-holding daemon → single static binary for first-class self-host; cheap goroutine-per-SSE-connection). Beat **Kotlin/Spring Boot** (JVM footprint + WebFlux-vs-blocking-SQLite mismatch fights self-host) and **Bun/TS** (viable, but Go won on expertise + SSE maturity). **OpenTUI** ruled out — it's a TUI renderer, not a backend.
- **HTTP framework: Gin** — for the webhook/REST surface + middleware; SSE remains a manual streaming handler, with buffering middleware exempted on `/sse/...`.

Both recorded in **ADR-0001** (now argues Go-vs-Cloudflare *and* Go-vs-Kotlin/Spring/Bun) and reflected in the README Hub section.

### Example using datasource

Source: https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-collector-contrib/blob/main/receiver/sqlqueryreceiver/README.md

Configuration for the SQL Query Receiver using a 'datasource' string to connect to a PostgreSQL database, including a logs query with tracking and a metrics query.

```yaml
receivers:
  sqlquery:
    driver: postgres
    datasource: "host=localhost port=5432 user=postgres password=s3cr3t sslmode=disable"
    queries:
      - sql: "select * from my_logs where log_id > $$1"
        tracking_start_value: "10000"
        tracking_column: log_id
        logs:
          - body_column: log_body
      - sql: "select count(*) as count, genre from movie group by genre"
        metrics:
          - metric_name: movie.genres
            value_column: "count"
```

--------------------------------

### Example Config

Source: https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-collector-contrib/blob/main/exporter/clickhouseexporter/README.md

Configuration for the ClickHouse exporter using native protocol without TLS, demonstrating database and table creation, TTL, compression, and retry settings.

```yaml
receivers:
  examplereceiver:
exporters:
  clickhouse:
    endpoint: tcp://127.0.0.1:9000?dial_timeout=10s
    database: otel
    async_insert: true
    ttl: 72h
    compress: lz4
    create_schema: true
    logs_table_name: otel_logs
    traces_table_name: otel_traces
    timeout: 5s
    metrics_tables:
      gauge: 
        name: "otel_metrics_gauge"
      sum: 
        name: "otel_metrics_sum"
      summary: 
        name: "otel_metrics_summary"
      histogram: 
        name: "otel_metrics_histogram"
      exponential_histogram: 
        name: "otel_metrics_exp_histogram"
    retry_on_failure:
      enabled: true
      initial_interval: 5s
      max_interval: 30s
      max_elapsed_time: 300s
    # cluster_name: my_cluster
    # table_engine:
    #   name: ReplicatedMergeTree
    #   params:
service:
  pipelines:
    logs:
      receivers: [ examplereceiver ]
      exporters: [ clickhouse ]
```

### Example OTEL Collector Configuration

Source: https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-collector-contrib/blob/main/exporter/clickhouseexporter/example/otel-collector-config.yml

Real-world example showing how to configure the ClickHouse exporter for traces and logs with database/table names, TTL, compression, retry policy, and sending queue batch settings. Demonstrates pipelines for both logs and traces data types.

```yaml
exporters:
  clickhouse:
    endpoint: tcp://clickhouse:9000
    database: otel
    logs_table_name: otel_logs
    traces_table_name: otel_traces
    ttl: 12h
    timeout: 10s
    sending_queue:
      queue_size: 100
    retry_on_failure:
      enabled: true
      initial_interval: 5s
      max_interval: 30s
      max_elapsed_time: 300s
    username: default
    password: default

service:
  pipelines:
    logs:
      receivers: [ fluentforward, otlp ]
      processors: [ memory_limiter, resource_detection/system, resource, batch ]
      exporters: [ clickhouse ]
    traces:
      receivers: [ otlp ]
      processors: [ memory_limiter, resource_detection/system, resource, batch ]
      exporters: [ clickhouse ]
```

### Docker Compose Setup with ClickHouse

Source: https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-collector-contrib/blob/main/exporter/clickhouseexporter/example/docker-compose.yml

Complete docker-compose configuration showing ClickHouse server setup, OTel collector with exporter, telemetry generators for traces (telemetrygen) and logs (flog), and Grafana visualization. Demonstrates network configuration, health checks, and data flow from generators through collector to ClickHouse.

```yaml
version: "3"

networks:
  otel-clickhouse:

services:
  otelcollector:
    build:
      context: ../../../local
      dockerfile: ../exporter/clickhouseexporter/example/Dockerfile
    container_name: otel
    command:
      - "--config=/etc/otel-collector-config.yml"
    volumes:
      - ./otel-collector-config.yml:/etc/otel-collector-config.yml
    ports:
      - "4317:4317"   # otlp receiver
      - "24224:24224" # fluentforwarder
    depends_on:
      clickhouse:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - otel-clickhouse

  clickhouse:
    image: clickhouse/clickhouse-server:latest
    ports:
      - "9000:9000"
      - "8123:8123"
    healthcheck:
      test: wget --no-verbose --tries=1 --spider http://localhost:8123/ping || exit 1
    networks:
      - otel-clickhouse
    environment:
      - CLICKHOUSE_USER=default
      - CLICKHOUSE_PASSWORD=default

  telemetrygen:
    build:
      context: ../../../cmd/telemetrygen/
    command:
      - traces
      - --otlp-endpoint=otelcollector:4317
      - --otlp-insecure
      - --rate=100
      - --duration=10000h
    networks:
      - otel-clickhouse
    depends_on:
      - otelcollector

  flog:
    image: mingrammer/flog:0.4.3
    command: [ "--format=json", "--loop", "--delay=100ms"]
    networks:
      - otel-clickhouse
    depends_on:
      - otelcollector
    logging:
      driver: fluentd
      options:
        fluentd-async-connect: "true"
        fluentd-sub-second-precision: "true"
```

### ClickHouse Exporter

Source: https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-collector-contrib/blob/main/exporter/clickhouseexporter/README.md

This exporter supports sending OpenTelemetry data to ClickHouse. ClickHouse is an open-source, high performance columnar OLAP database management system for real-time analytics using SQL. Throughput can be measured in rows per second or megabytes per second. If the data is placed in the page cache, a query that is not too complex is processed on modern hardware at a speed of approximately 2-10 GB/s of uncompressed data on a single server. If 10 bytes of columns are extracted, the speed is expected to be around 100-200 million rows per second.
```

### Create a hypertable with columnstore enabled

Source: https://github.com/timescale/timescaledb/blob/main/README.md

SQL command to create a hypertable named 'sensor_data' with time, sensor_id, temperature, humidity, and pressure columns, enabling TimescaleDB hypertable functionality.

```sql
-- Create a hypertable with automatic columnstore
CREATE TABLE sensor_data (
    time TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    sensor_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    temperature DOUBLE PRECISION,
    humidity DOUBLE PRECISION,
    pressure DOUBLE PRECISION
) WITH (
    tsdb.hypertable
);
```

--------------------------------

### Create app_events table with UUIDv7 partitioning

Source: https://github.com/timescale/timescaledb/blob/main/docs/getting-started/events-uuidv7/README.md

SQL statement to create the app_events hypertable with UUIDv7 partitioning on event_id and segmentation by user_id. It notes that no separate timestamp column is needed and no PRIMARY KEY is used to allow direct compression during COPY.

```sql
-- Create the app_events table with UUIDv7 partitioning
-- Note: No separate timestamp column needed - the timestamp is embedded in the UUIDv7!
-- Note: No PRIMARY KEY to allow direct compress during COPY
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS app_events (
    event_id UUID NOT NULL,
    user_id UUID NOT NULL,
    session_id UUID NOT NULL,
    event_type TEXT NOT NULL,
    event_name TEXT,
    device_type TEXT,
    country_code TEXT,
    category TEXT,
    page_path TEXT,
    referrer TEXT,
    viewport_width INTEGER,
    element_id TEXT,
    position_x INTEGER,
    position_y INTEGER,
    product_id TEXT,
    quantity INTEGER,
    revenue_cents INTEGER
) WITH (
    tsdb.hypertable,
    tsdb.partition_column = 'event_id',
    tsdb.segmentby = 'user_id'
);

-- Create index on event_id for lookups (not unique to allow direct compress)
CREATE INDEX idx_app_events_event_id ON app_events(event_id);
```

--------------------------------

### Create the optimized hypertable

Source: https://github.com/timescale/timescaledb/blob/main/docs/getting-started/nyc-taxi/README.md

This SQL statement creates a 'trips' table optimized for NYC Taxi data, enabling automatic time-based partitioning, columnstore, segmentation, and ordering.

```sql
-- Create the hypertable with optimal settings for NYC Taxi data
-- This automatically enables columnstore for fast analytical queries
CREATE TABLE trips (
    vendor_id TEXT,
    pickup_boroname VARCHAR,
    pickup_datetime TIMESTAMP WITHOUT TIME ZONE NOT NULL,
    dropoff_datetime TIMESTAMP WITHOUT TIME ZONE NOT NULL,
    passenger_count NUMERIC,
    trip_distance NUMERIC,
    pickup_longitude NUMERIC,
    pickup_latitude NUMERIC,
    rate_code INTEGER,
    dropoff_longitude NUMERIC,
    dropoff_latitude NUMERIC,
    payment_type VARCHAR,
    fare_amount NUMERIC,
    extra NUMERIC,
    mta_tax NUMERIC,
    tip_amount NUMERIC,
    tolls_amount NUMERIC,
    improvement_surcharge NUMERIC,
    total_amount NUMERIC
) WITH (
    tsdb.hypertable,
    tsdb.partition_column='pickup_datetime',
    tsdb.enable_columnstore=true,
    tsdb.segmentby='pickup_boroname',
    tsdb.orderby='pickup_datetime DESC'
);

-- Create indexes 
CREATE INDEX idx_trips_pickup_time ON trips (pickup_datetime DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_trips_borough_time ON trips (pickup_boroname, pickup_datetime DESC);
```
